A logic for default deontic reasoning
Mario Piazza, Andrea Sabatini

Abstract
. In many real-life settings, agents must navigate dynamic environments while reasoning under incomplete information and acting on a corpus of unstable, context-dependent, and often conflicting norms. We introduce a general, non-modal, proof-theoretic framework for deontic reasoning grounded in default logic. Its central feature is the notion of controlled sequent – a sequent annotated with sets of formulas (control sets) that prescribe what should or should not be entailed by the formulas in the antecedent. When combined with distinct extra-logical rules representing defaults and norms, these control sets record the conditions and constraints governing their applicability, thereby enabling local soundness checks for derived sequents. We prove that controlled sequent calculi satisfies admissibility of contraction and non-analytic cuts, and we establish their strong completeness with respect to credulous consequence in default theories and normative systems. Finally, we illustrate in depth how controlled sequent calculi provide a flexible and expressive basis for resolving deontic conflicts and capturing dynamic deontic notions via appropriate extra-logical rules.



1.
Introduction

Norms – whether expressed as parental recommendations, military orders, traffic regulations, religious commandments, workplace policies, or self-imposed codes of conduct – present a logical puzzle. On one hand, their observance or violation is a binary matter, naturally modeled by the semantics of classical propositional logic. On the other hand, norms are often unstable and context-dependent: the introduction of a new norm can override or invalidate previous ones, which places their behavior squarely within the realm of non-monotonic logic. To take an example from the first book of Plato’s
Republic
, the norm “One ought to return what one has borrowed” may be overridden in exceptional circumstances – for instance, if the person who lent a weapon has since gone mad. The norm is not monotonic: it applies by default, but can be defeated by relevant contextual changes, just as the generalization “dogs bark” is overridden by the more specific fact that “Basenjis do not bark”. Finally, norms interact in complex ways, sometimes generating conflicts, even though – being commands rather than declarative statements – they do not possess truth values in the traditional logical sense.

As is well known, from a logical standpoint, the most traditional approach to normative reasoning is provided by the family of deontic logics, which extend classical modal logic by introducing specific operators that enable the formal representation of norms, obligations and permissions within a Hilbert-style system, sequent calculus, or one of their variants
[
45
,
15
,
26
,
9
]
. However, standard deontic logics suffer from several well-documented limitations, including difficulties in addressing deontic paradoxes (such as the Good Samaritan paradox
[
35
]
or contrary-to-duty obligations
[
8
,
11
,
43
]
), and limited flexibility in accommodating context-sensitive, specificity-based or dynamic normative structures. By contrast, about 25 years ago a different proposal was developed in terms of the so-called Input/Output Logics (I/O logics)
[
21
,
22
,
23
]
. Such systems offer a non-modal alternative by treating conditional obligations as inference rules, explicitly separating input conditions (I) from outputs (O) expressing what is obliged, prohibited or permitted under those conditions. This framework emphasizes what ought to be done in a given situation, without treating obligations as modal truths. However -- while more adaptable and better suited for modeling conditional and defeasible norms -- I/O logics remain at a relatively early stage in the development of robust and comprehensive proof systems
1
1

1

For a recent proposal based on a hypersequent framework, see
[
32
]
.
.

The novel perspective proposed in this work differs significantly from both of these paradigms: it is modular, computationally more efficient, and provides fine-grained control over inference steps. These features allow for more transparent, scalable, and adaptive normative reasoning, particularly in complex or multi-layered settings.
Our aim is to develop a general and non-modal proof-theoretic framework for norms from the standpoint of a rational or artificial agent navigating a dynamic environment. This environment is stable enough to support expected obligations and prohibitions while remaining open and adaptable to new, potentially conflicting norms. As evidence of such flexibility, we will consider a wide range of concrete deontic scenario.

Moreover, the framework introduced articulates the interplay between normative reasoning and default reasoning. The latter is a type of non-monotonic reasoning that enables the derivation of plausible conclusions in contexts of incomplete information and absent explicit contradictory evidence. Such conclusions are defeasible: they can be retracted when challenged by new information
[
38
,
25
,
2
]
. Default reasoning can be formalized by extending classical logic with a collection of
extra-logical axioms
– which represent the propositional contents of an ideal reasoner’s beliefs – and a set of
default rules
, which encode the inferential pathways that lead to defeasible yet consistent conclusions.
In adopting a
credulous
approach, the ideal reasoner accepts as many individually consistent beliefs as possible. When reasoning with norms, such credulity is not merely permissible but necessary: a skeptical stance would unduly inhibit normative inference by prematurely excluding viable obligations. The idea of connecting normative and default reasoning is not new, as it has been especially suggested by John Horty
[
19
,
18
]
. However, our approach is implemented beyond a purely programmatic level, by means of a proof-theoretic platform.

Technically, such implementation is based on the notion of
controlled
classical proof-system: a system built upon classical sequents augmented with a layer of extra-logical information, whose propagation is governed by the very structure of derivations.
In earlier work, controlled classical sequents
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathbf{S}}\Delta
were introduced to capture non-monotonic and paraconsistent behaviour within a classical framework
[
28
]
. The control set
\mathbf{S}
encodes extra-logical information that can selectively block the derivability of
\Delta
from
\Gamma

[
5
,
10
,
6
,
28
,
34
]
.
In the present paper, by contrast, controlled sequents are syntactic devices tailored specifically to represent defaults and norms over a classical base.
We adopt specific extra-logical rules to formalize defaults, as well as (un)conditional obligations and permissions: each sequent in the calculus is annotated with a
control pair

\langle\mathbf{T},\mathbf{S}\rangle
(written:
\Gamma\mathrel{\mkern 2.0mu\rule[0.0pt]{0.4pt}{12.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 3.14027pt\hskip-2.51389pt\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 2.51389pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\rule[0.0pt]{10.76385pt}{0.4pt}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-2.51389pt\raisebox{-5.80278pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{S}$}}\hskip-2.51389pt\hskip 2.51389pt}$}\hskip-2.51389pt\hskip-3.14027pt\raisebox{5.80278pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{T}$}}\hskip-3.14027pt\hskip 3.14027pt}\mkern 2.0mu}\Delta
), where
\mathbf{T}
and
\mathbf{S}
are sets of formulas that specify prescriptions on what should or should not be entailed by the formulas in the antecedent for an extra-logical rules to apply. To provide an initial sense of the approach advanced in this paper, we begin with an informal example.

Example 1.1
.

Let the extra-logical rule

\Gamma\mathrel{\mkern 2.0mu\rule[0.0pt]{0.4pt}{12.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 5.28606pt\hskip-4.65967pt\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 4.65967pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\rule[0.0pt]{10.76385pt}{0.4pt}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-4.65967pt\raisebox{-5.80278pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bf S_{1}}$}}\hskip-4.65967pt\hskip 4.65967pt}$}\hskip-4.65967pt\hskip-5.28606pt\raisebox{6.80278pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bf T_{1}}$}}\hskip-5.28606pt\hskip 5.28606pt}\mkern 2.0mu}p

\Gamma\mathrel{\mkern 2.0mu\rule[0.0pt]{0.4pt}{12.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 5.28606pt\hskip-4.65967pt\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 4.65967pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\rule[0.0pt]{10.76385pt}{0.4pt}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-4.65967pt\raisebox{-5.80278pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bf S_{2}}$}}\hskip-4.65967pt\hskip 4.65967pt}$}\hskip-4.65967pt\hskip-5.28606pt\raisebox{6.80278pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bf T_{2}}$}}\hskip-5.28606pt\hskip 5.28606pt}\mkern 2.0mu}q

\Gamma\mathrel{\mkern 2.0mu\rule[0.0pt]{0.4pt}{12.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 3.14027pt\hskip-2.51389pt\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 2.51389pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\rule[0.0pt]{10.76385pt}{0.4pt}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-2.51389pt\raisebox{-5.80278pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bf S}$}}\hskip-2.51389pt\hskip 2.51389pt}$}\hskip-2.51389pt\hskip-3.14027pt\raisebox{5.80278pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bf T}$}}\hskip-3.14027pt\hskip 3.14027pt}\mkern 2.0mu}r

stand for the normative statement: ‘If one prays and he is a male, one ought to wear Tefillin’ – where
{\bf T}={\bf T_{1}}\cup{\bf T_{2}}\cup\{\{p,q\}\}
. Let
s
stand for the (factual) statement ‘it is nighttime’. Now, consider the following extra-logical rule:

\Gamma\mathrel{\mkern 2.0mu\rule[0.0pt]{0.4pt}{12.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 5.28606pt\hskip-4.65967pt\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 4.65967pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\rule[0.0pt]{10.76385pt}{0.4pt}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-4.65967pt\raisebox{-5.80278pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bf S_{1}}$}}\hskip-4.65967pt\hskip 4.65967pt}$}\hskip-4.65967pt\hskip-5.28606pt\raisebox{6.80278pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bf T_{1}}$}}\hskip-5.28606pt\hskip 5.28606pt}\mkern 2.0mu}p

\Gamma\mathrel{\mkern 2.0mu\rule[0.0pt]{0.4pt}{12.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 5.28606pt\hskip-4.65967pt\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 4.65967pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\rule[0.0pt]{10.76385pt}{0.4pt}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-4.65967pt\raisebox{-5.80278pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bf S_{2}}$}}\hskip-4.65967pt\hskip 4.65967pt}$}\hskip-4.65967pt\hskip-5.28606pt\raisebox{6.80278pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bf T_{2}}$}}\hskip-5.28606pt\hskip 5.28606pt}\mkern 2.0mu}q

\Gamma\mathrel{\mkern 2.0mu\rule[0.0pt]{0.4pt}{12.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 3.86632pt\hskip-3.86632pt\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 3.86632pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\rule[0.0pt]{10.76385pt}{0.4pt}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-3.86632pt\raisebox{-6.7956pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bf S^{\prime}}$}}\hskip-3.86632pt\hskip 3.86632pt}$}\hskip-3.86632pt\hskip-3.14027pt\raisebox{6.7956pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bf T}$}}\hskip-3.14027pt\hskip 3.86632pt}\mkern 2.0mu}r

where
{\bf S^{\prime}}={\bf S}\cup\{\{s\}\}
. Such extra-logical rule expresses the normative claim: ‘if one prays, then one ought to wear the Tefillin provided that one is male, unless it is nighttime’. This formulation captures the rule governing the wearing of Tefillin during prayer, taking both gender and temporal constraints into account.

The paper is structured as follows. Section
2
contains the notions and results concerning normative systems and default logics that we will subsequently employ. In particular, we introduce a notion of
deontic extension
for systems of obligations and permissions, analogous to the notion of Łukasiewicz extension for defaults. In Section
3
we introduce a uniform proof-theoretic platform for defaults, obligations and permissions based on controlled sequents. Controlled sequent calculi enjoy admissibility of contraction and non-analytic cut, and proofs without non-analytic cuts exhibit a weakened form of analyticity. We establish that controlled sequent calculi are strongly complete with respect to credulous consequence based on Łukasiewicz extensions (for defaults) and deontic extensions (for obligations and permissions). Hence, we show that controlled sequent calculi enable the formalization of weak versions of cumulative transitivity and cautious monotony for the underlying credulous consequence relations. Section
4
gathers a number of examples of deontic scenarios, observed through the
lens
of controlled calculi. Lastly, Section
5
outlines directions for future research.

2.1.
The
\overline{\overline{\mathsf{G4^{pn}}}}
calculus

We work with a propositional language containing a denumerable set of atoms
p,q,r,\ldots
, the unary connective
\neg
, and the binary connectives
\wedge,\vee
for conjunction and disjunction. Capital Latin letters
A,B,C,\ldots
range over formulas, while capital Greek letters
\Gamma,\Delta,\Pi,\Sigma,\ldots
denote finite multisets of formulas. We use
\Theta,\Lambda,\ldots
for finite multisets of
literals
, that is, atoms
p,q,r,\ldots
and their negations
\neg p,\neg q,\neg r,\ldots
.
For contexts
\Theta=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{k},\neg q_{1},\ldots,\neg q_{m}\}
and
\Gamma=\{A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}\}
, we set:

\Theta^{\bot}=\{\neg p_{1},\ldots,\neg p_{k},q_{1},\ldots,q_{m}\}\hskip 13.0pt\bigwedge\Gamma=A_{1}\wedge A_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge A_{n}\hskip 13.0pt\bigvee\Gamma=A_{1}\vee A_{2}\vee\cdots\vee A_{n}

For
\Theta={A}
, we write
A^{\bot}
for
\Theta^{\bot}
. For
\Gamma=\emptyset
, we stipulate
\Gamma^{\perp}=\Gamma
,
\bigwedge\Gamma=\top
, and
\bigvee\Gamma=\bot
, where
\top
and
\bot
denote an arbitrary tautology and contradiction, respectively. The
logical complexity

\mathrm{C}(A)
of a formula
A
is defined as follows:
\mathrm{C}(A)=1
if
A
is a literal;
\mathrm{C}(A)=\mathrm{C}(B)+\mathrm{C}(C)+1
if
A
has the form
B\otimes C
; and
\mathrm{C}(A)=\mathrm{C}(\neg B)+\mathrm{C}(\neg C)+1
if
A
has the form
\neg(B\otimes C)
, where
\otimes\in\{\wedge,\vee\}
. This extends to multisets
\Gamma=A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}
by setting
\mathrm{C}(\Gamma)=\mathrm{C}(A_{1})+\ldots+\mathrm{C}(A_{n})
.

We consider Gentzen-style sequents
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
as well as
antisequents

\Gamma\dashv\Delta
, where
\Gamma\dashv\Delta
is valid if and only if
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
is invalid
[
14
,
13
]
. In classical logic, an antisequent is valid precisely when some Boolean valuation makes every formula in
\Gamma
true and every formula in
\Delta
false.

The system
\overline{\overline{\mathsf{G4^{pn}}}}
for classical propositional logic is a modification of the system
\overline{\overline{\mathsf{G4}}}
. The latter, imported from
[
29
]
, treats logical contexts as multisets of formulas and is obtained by extending Kleene’s original
{\sf G4}
system
[
20
, pp. 289-290, p. 306]
with the complementary axiom

\overline{ax}

\Theta\dashv\Lambda

, where
\Theta\cap\Lambda=\varnothing
, together with distinct rules for antisequents.
The system
\overline{\overline{\mathsf{G4^{pn}}}}
retains the rules for
\neg
from
\overline{\overline{\mathsf{G4}}}
via a broader class of
ax
instances, and further incorporates rules for
\neg\wedge
,
\neg\vee
, and
\neg\neg
— hence the superscript
{\sf pn}
for ‘primitive negation’. To speak uniformly about sequents (
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
) and antisequents (
\Gamma\dashv\Delta
) whenever the distinction is immaterial and they are taken independently of any specific deduction-refutation system deriving them, we use the term ‘
\ast
-sequent’ and write the generic form
\Gamma\mathbin{\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}}\Delta
.
The measure
\mathrm{C}
extends to any
\ast
-sequent
\Gamma\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Delta
by setting
\mathrm{C}(\Gamma\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Delta)=\mathrm{C}(\Gamma)+\mathrm{C}(\Delta)
.

A
\overline{\overline{\mathsf{G4^{pn}}}}
derivation
\pi
may terminate either in a sequent
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
or in an antisequent
\Gamma\dashv\Delta
. In the former case, we say that
\pi
is a
proof
of
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
; in the latter,
\pi
is a
refutation
of
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
.
Any
\ast
-sequent
\Gamma\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Delta
can be decomposed into a set of atomic
\ast
-sequents by applying, bottom-up, the rules
L\wedge,R\wedge,L\vee,R\vee,L\neg\wedge,R\neg\wedge,L\neg\vee,R\neg\vee,L\neg\neg,R\neg\neg
from Figure
1
, with
\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}
in place of
\vdash
, until every leaf of the resulting tree is an atomic
\ast
-sequent – namely, one containing only literals.

We now highlight two crucial features of the
\overline{\overline{\mathsf{G4^{pn}}}}
proof system:

Proposition 2.1
.

\overline{\overline{\mathsf{G4^{pn}}}}
proves (refutes)
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
if and
only if the formula
\neg\bigwedge\Gamma\vee\bigvee\Delta
is classically valid ( invalid).

Proposition 2.2
.

Maximal
\overline{\overline{\mathsf{G4^{pn}}}}
-decomposition yields a unique set of atomic
\ast
-sequents.

Proof.

We argue as in the proof of the same property for
\overline{\overline{\mathsf{G4}}}
in
[
4
,
29
]
.
∎

Proposition
2.2
allows us to refer directly to the unique set of atomic
\ast
-sequents associated with any given
\ast
-sequent
\Gamma\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Delta
, since this decomposition is independent of the particular derivation producing it. For any set of atomic
\ast
-sequents
\mathcal{C}
, we say that
\mathcal{C}
is
closed under Negation
whenever
\Theta_{1},\Lambda_{2}^{\bot}\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Theta_{2}^{\bot},\Lambda_{1}
belongs to
\mathcal{C}
if
\Theta_{1},\Theta_{2}\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Lambda_{2},\Lambda_{1}
belongs to
\mathcal{C}
. We write
\mathsf{top}(\Gamma\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Delta)
for the closure under Negation of the atomic
\ast
-sequents associated with
\Gamma\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Delta
. We write
\mathsf{top_{cp}}(\Gamma\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Delta)
for the subset of those atomic
\ast
-sequents
\Theta\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Lambda
in
\mathsf{top}(\Gamma\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Delta)
such that
\Theta\cap\Lambda=\varnothing
and neither
\Theta
nor
\Lambda
does not contains both an atom and its negation; the subscript
{\sf cp}
stands for ‘complementary’. Finally,
\mathsf{top_{r}}(\Gamma\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Delta)
denotes the complementary
\ast
-sequents of the form
\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Lambda
in
\mathsf{top_{cp}}(\Gamma\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Delta)
– where the subscript
{\sf r}
stands for ‘right-sided’.

Example 2.1
.

This is a decomposition-tree of
\neg(p\wedge q)\vee r\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}s\vee\neg t
:

\neg(p\wedge q)\vee r\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}s\vee\neg t

R\vee

\neg(p\wedge q)\vee r\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}s,\neg t

L\vee

\neg(p\wedge q)\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}s,\neg t

L\neg\wedge

\neg p\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}s,\neg t

\neg q\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}s,\neg t

r\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}s,\neg t

Hence, we have that
\mathsf{top}(\neg(p\wedge q)\vee r\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}s\vee\neg t)=\{\neg p\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}s,\neg t\,;\,\neg q\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}s,\neg \,;\,\neg ,
\mathsf{top_{cp}}(\neg(p\wedge q)\vee r\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}s\vee\neg t)=\mathsf{top}(\neg(p\wedge q)\vee r\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}s\vee\neg t)
and
\mathsf{top_{r}}(\neg(p\wedge q)\vee r\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}s\vee\neg t)=\{\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}p,s,\neg t\,;\,\ \scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}s,\neg t,q\,;\,\ \scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\neg r,s,\neg t\}
.

For any set
\mathcal{C}
of atomic
\ast
-sequents, we say that
\mathcal{C}
is closed under Contraction whenever

-

if
A,A,\Theta\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Lambda
or
A,\Theta\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Lambda,A^{\bot}
is in
\mathcal{C}
, then
A,\Theta\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Lambda
is in
\mathcal{C}
;

-

if
\Theta\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Lambda,A,A
or
A^{\bot},\Theta\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Lambda,A
is in
\mathcal{C}
, then
\Theta\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Lambda,A
is in
\mathcal{C}
.

Furthermore, we say that
\mathcal{C}
is closed under Cut when
\Phi,\Theta\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Lambda,\Psi
is in
\mathcal{C}
if one of the following condition holds:

-

\Theta\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Lambda,A
and
A,\Phi\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Psi
;

-

\Theta\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Lambda,A
and
\Phi\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Psi,A^{\bot}
;

-

A^{\bot},\Theta\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Lambda
and
A,\Phi\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Psi
2
2

2

When employing
\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}
in the definition of closure under Cut, we do not mean to imply that closure under Cut preserves refutability within any particular deduction-refutation system; indeed, it clearly does not. Rather, our aim is to provide a formulation of closure under Cut that abstracts away from the commitments of any specific deduction-refutation framework.
.

We write
\mathsf{top}^{\#}(\Gamma\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Delta)
to refer to the set of atomic
\ast
-sequents which is obtained from
\mathsf{top}(\Gamma\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Delta)
by maximal application of the following steps (cf.
[
33
, p. 9]
):

(i)

start with
\mathcal{C}_{0}=\mathsf{top}(\Gamma\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Delta)
;

(ii)

take the closure under Contraction of
\mathcal{C}_{n}
;

(iii)

if either
\Theta\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Lambda,A
and
A,\Phi\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Psi
, or
A^{\bot},\Theta\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Lambda
and
A,\Phi\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Psi
, or
A,\Theta\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Lambda
and
A^{\bot},\Phi\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Psi
belong to
\mathcal{C}_{n}
, and
\Phi,\Theta\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Lambda,\Psi
does not belong to
\mathcal{C}_{n}
, then take
\mathcal{C}_{n+1}=\mathcal{C}_{n}\cup\{\Phi,\Theta\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Lambda,\Psi\}
.

We use
\mathsf{top_{cp}^{\#}}(\Gamma\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Delta)
to denote the set of complementary
\ast
-sequents in
\mathsf{top}^{\#}(\Gamma\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Delta)
. Finally, we employ
\mathsf{top_{r}^{\#}}(\Gamma\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Delta)
to denote the smallest set containing all the complementary
\ast
-sequents in
\mathsf{top}^{\#}(\Gamma\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\Delta)
whose left-hand side is empty.

Example 2.2
.

Consider the
\ast
-sequent
\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}(\neg p\vee q)\wedge(\neg q\vee r)

.

Hence,

\scriptsize\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{2.41112pt}{$\rule[-4.52083pt]{0.33452pt}{9.04166pt}\rule[-0.16725pt]{5.7277pt}{0.33452pt}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{-1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-5.7277pt\raisebox{1.37277pt}{\makebox[5.7277pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ast}$}}}$}}}\neg \}
.

2.2.
Default theories

A
default
is a domain-specific inference rule of the form

\dfrac{B:C_{1},\ldots,C_{n}}{D}

(1)

where
B
is the
prerequisite
, the non-contradictory formulas
C_{1},\ldots,C_{n}
are the
justifications
and the non-tautological formula
D
is the
conclusion
of the default. Its interpretation is that if
B
is proved, then
D
is provable, so long as the formulas
\neg C_{1},\ldots,\neg C_{n}
are not provable. We say that a default rule is
triggered
whenever its prerequisite is proved.

If
n=1
, then a default rule of the form (
1
) is
normal
if and only if
\overline{\overline{\mathsf{G4^{pn}}}}
proves both the sequent
C_{1}\vdash D
and
D\vdash C_{1}
and
semi-normal
if and only if
\overline{\overline{\mathsf{G4^{pn}}}}
proves
C_{1}\vdash D
. For convenience, when discussing normal default rules, we only focus on those for which
C_{1}=D
.

A
default theory
is a pair
\langle\mathcal{W},\mathcal{D}\rangle
, where
\mathcal{W}
is a finite, consistent set of extra-logical axioms and
\mathcal{D}
is a finite, non-empty set of default rules. A default theory
\langle\mathcal{W},\mathcal{D}\rangle
is said to be normal if and only if all the defaults in
\mathcal{D}
are normal. We will use
req(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}),just(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
and
concl(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
to refer to the set of prerequisites, justifications and conclusions, respectively, of the defaults in any
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
.

A
modified extension
(called also
Łukaszewicz extension
) is a set of formulas derivable from
\mathcal{W}
by classical logic and the maximal application of default rules in
\mathcal{D}
whose consistency condition holds (both before and after their being triggered) relatively to a simultaneously defined support set (
[
2
, pp. 75-76]
,
[
46
]
). Specifically, we say that
\langle\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F}\rangle
is a modified extension of
\langle\mathcal{W},\mathcal{D}\rangle
if and only if
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{F}
are quasi-inductively defined as follows:

\mathcal{E}^{0}=\mathcal{W}\ \ \ \ \ \mathcal{F}^{0}=\varnothing

\mathcal{E}^{k+1}=Cn(\mathcal{E}^{k})\cup\{concl(\mathcal{D^{\prime}})\mid\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{D},req(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{k},A\in(\mathcal{F}\cup just(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}))\Rightarrow\neg A\not\in Cn(\mathcal{E}\cup concl(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}))\}

\mathcal{F}^{k+1}=\mathcal{F}^{k}\cup\{just(\mathcal{D^{\prime}})\mid\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{D},req(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{k},A\in(\mathcal{F}\cup just(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}))\Rightarrow\neg A\not\in Cn(\mathcal{E}\cup concl(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}))\}

\mathcal{E}=\bigcup\limits_{i=0}^{\omega}\mathcal{E}^{i}\ \ \ \ \ \mathcal{F}=\bigcup\limits_{i=0}^{\omega}\mathcal{F}^{i}

Example 2.3
.

Let
\langle\mathcal{W},\mathcal{D}\rangle
be defined as follows:

\mathcal{W}=\varnothing

\mathcal{D}=\Big\{\dfrac{\top:\neg q\wedge p}{p}\ ,\ \dfrac{\top:\neg r\wedge q}{q}\ ,\ \dfrac{\top:\neg p\wedge r}{r}\Big\}

\langle\mathcal{W},\mathcal{D}\rangle
has multiple modified extensions – namely,
\langle\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1}\rangle=\langle Cn(\{p\}),\{\neg q\wedge p\}\rangle
,
\langle\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{F}_{2}\rangle=\langle Cn(\{q\})
,
\{\neg r\wedge q\}\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{E}_{3},\mathcal{F}_{3}\rangle=\langle Cn(\{r\}),\{\neg p\wedge r\}\rangle
.

If
\langle\mathcal{W},\mathcal{D}\rangle
is a default theory, then
A
is a
modified credulous consequence
(in short, an
m
-credulous consequence) of
\mathcal{W}
if and only if
A
belongs to at least one modified extension of
\langle\mathcal{W},\mathcal{D}\rangle
. Intuitively, the credulous agent commits to as many individually consistent beliefs as possible.

It is straightforward to see that the modified credulous consequence relation is supraclassical yet
non-monotonic
: conclusions it supports may fail to persist when arbitrary premises are added
[
24
]
. Nevertheless, it satisfies the key property of
semi-monotonicity
: no modified credulous consequence of
\mathcal{W}
can be invalidated by adding new default rules:

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
\langle\mathcal{W},\mathcal{D}\rangle
be a default theory, and
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. For any modified extension
\langle\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F}\rangle
of
\langle\mathcal{W},\mathcal{D}\rangle
there exists (at least) one modified extension
\langle\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\rangle
of
\langle\mathcal{W},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\rangle
such that
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

For a proof see
[
46
, pp. 12-13]
.
∎

2.3.
Obligations and permissions as extra-logical rules

We consider a
constrained conditional obligation
(permission) as a domain-specific inference rule of the form

\dfrac{B:C_{1},\ldots,C_{n}}{D}

(2)

where
B
is the
conditions
, the non-contradictory formulas
C_{1},\ldots,C_{n}
are the
constraints
and the non-tautological formula
D
is the
conclusion
of the constrained conditional obligation (permission, respectively). Its interpretation is that if
B
is/ought to be (is/is permitted to be) the case, then
D
ought to be (is permitted to be) the case, so long as
C_{1},\ldots,C_{n}
ought not (are not permitted to, respectively) be the case. We say that a conditional obligation (permission) has a
factual condition
if the intended interpretation of the prerequisite is factual: if
B

is the case
, then
D
ought (is permitted) to be the case. On the other hand, we say that a conditional obligation (permission) has a
deontic condition
if the intended interpretation of the prerequisite is deontic, non-factual: if
B

ought (is permitted) to be the case
, then
D
ought (is permitted) to be the case. If
n=1
, we say that a constrained conditional obligation (permission) of the form (
2
) is
normal
whenever
C_{1}=D
3
3

3

Let us emphasize that, whereas
[
22
]
considers a fixed set of constraints for all conditional obligations, we expand this perspective by allowing each conditional obligation (and each permission) to be associated with its own specific set of constraints. In addition, we address defaults and conditional norms within a unified framework, thereby enabling default reasoning over the factual conditions that govern obligations and permissions.
. If
B
is logically equivalent to
\top
, a rule of the form
2
is a constrained
unconditional
obligation. We say that an obligation (permission) is
triggered
whenever its condition is proved.

An
obligation system
is a pair
\langle\mathcal{W}^{\sf o},\mathcal{O}\rangle
, where
\mathcal{W}^{\sf o}
is a finite and consistent set of extra-logical axioms – expressing unconstrained, unconditional obligations – and
\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}^{\sf f}\cup\mathcal{O}^{\sf d}
, where
\mathcal{O}^{\sf f}
is a finite, non-empty set of constrained (un)conditional obligations with factual conditions and
\mathcal{O}^{\sf d}
is a finite set of constrained (un)conditional obligations with deontic conditions.
Similarly, a
permission system
is a pair
\langle\mathcal{W}^{\sf p},\mathcal{P}\rangle
, where
\mathcal{W}^{\sf p}
is a finite, consistent set of extra-logical axioms – expressing unconstrained, unconditional permissions – and
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}^{\sf f}\cup\mathcal{P}^{\sf d}
, where
\mathcal{P}^{\sf f}
is a finite, non-empty set of constrained (un)conditional permissions with factual conditions and
\mathcal{P}^{\sf d}
is a finite set of constrained (un)conditional permissions with deontic conditions.

A
normative system

\mathcal{N}
is a pair
\langle\langle\mathcal{W}^{\sf o},\mathcal{O}\rangle,\langle\mathcal{W}^{\sf p},\mathcal{P}\rangle\rangle
, where
(i)

\langle\mathcal{W}^{\sf o},\mathcal{O}\rangle
is an obligation system,
(ii)

\langle\mathcal{W}^{\sf p},\mathcal{P}\rangle
is a permission system and
(iii)

\mathcal{W}^{\sf o}\subseteq\mathcal{W}^{\sf p}
and
\mathcal{O}^{\star}\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{\star}
with
\star\in\{{\sf f},{\sf d}\}
. A normative system
\langle\langle\mathcal{W}^{\sf o},\mathcal{O}\rangle,\langle\mathcal{W}^{\sf p},\mathcal{P}\rangle\rangle
is said to be normal if and only if all the permissions in
\mathcal{P}
are normal. We shall use
cond(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})
,
constr(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})
and
concl(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})
to refer to the set of conditions, constraints and conclusions, respectively, of the obligations (permissions) in any
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{O}
(
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
, respectively).

Let
\langle\mathcal{W},\mathcal{D}\rangle
be a default theory and
\mathcal{N}
a normative system. Given a modified extension
\langle\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F}\rangle
of
\langle\mathcal{W},\mathcal{D}\rangle
, an
obligation
(
permission
)
extension
is a set of formulas derivable from
\mathcal{E},\mathcal{W}^{\sf o}
and
\mathcal{W}^{\sf p}
by classical logic and maximal application of the obligations (permissions, respectively) in
\mathcal{N}
whose consistency condition holds (both before and after their being triggered) relatively to a simultaneously defined support set. Specifically, we say that
\langle\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{E}},\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{E}}\rangle
is an obligation extension of
\langle\langle\mathcal{W},\mathcal{D}\rangle,\mathcal{N}\rangle
if and only if
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{E}}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{E}}
are quasi-inductively defined as follows:

\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{E}}^{0}=\mathcal{W}^{\sf o}\ \ \ \ \ \mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{E}}^{0}=\varnothing

\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{E}}^{k+1}=Cn(\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{E}}^{k})\,\cup\,\left\{concl(\mathcal{O}^{\sf f}_{x}),concl(\mathcal{O}^{\sf d}_{x})\;\middle|\;\begin{array}[]{l}\mathcal{O}^{\sf f}_{x}\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{\sf f},\mathcal{O}^{\sf d}_{x}\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{\sf d},\\
req(\mathcal{O}^{\sf f}_{x})\subseteq\mathcal{E},req(\mathcal{O}^{\sf d}_{x})\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{E}}^{k},\\[2.0pt]
A\in(\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{E}}\cup just(\mathcal{O}^{\sf f}_{x}))\Rightarrow\neg A\not\in Cn(\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{E}}\cup concl(\mathcal{O}_{x}^{\sf f})\cup concl(\mathcal{O}_{x}^{\sf d}))\end{array}\right\}

\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{E}}^{k+1}=\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{E}}^{k}\,\cup\,\left\{just(\mathcal{O}^{\sf f}_{x}),just(\mathcal{O}^{\sf d}_{x})\;\middle|\;\begin{array}[]{l}\mathcal{O}^{\sf f}_{x}\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{\sf f},\mathcal{O}^{\sf d}_{x}\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{\sf d},\\
req(\mathcal{O}^{\sf f}_{x})\subseteq\mathcal{E},req(\mathcal{O}^{\sf d}_{x})\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{E}}^{k},\\[2.0pt]
A\in(\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{E}}\cup just(\mathcal{O}^{\sf f}_{x}))\Rightarrow\neg A\not\in Cn(\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{E}}\cup concl(\mathcal{O}_{x}^{\sf f})\cup concl(\mathcal{O}_{x}^{\sf d}))\end{array}\right\}

\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{E}}=\bigcup\limits_{i=0}^{\omega}\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{E}}^{i}\ \ \ \ \ \mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{E}}=\bigcup\limits_{i=0}^{\omega}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{E}}^{i}

A permission extension
\langle\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{E}},\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{E}}\rangle
of
\langle\langle\mathcal{W},\mathcal{D}\rangle,\mathcal{N}\rangle
is defined as an obligation extension of
\langle\langle\mathcal{W},\mathcal{D}\rangle,\mathcal{N}\rangle
, except for the fact that we replace
\mathcal{W}^{\sf o}
with
\mathcal{W}^{\sf p}
and
\mathcal{O}_{x}^{\star}
with
\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\star}
, where
\star\in\{{\sf f},{\sf d}\}
. We say that
\langle\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{E}},\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{E}}\rangle
is a deontic extension of
\langle\langle\mathcal{W},\mathcal{D}\rangle,\mathcal{N}\rangle
if
\langle\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{E}},\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{E}}\rangle
is an obligation extension or a permission extension of
\langle\langle\mathcal{W},\mathcal{D}\rangle,\mathcal{N}\rangle
.

If
\langle\langle\mathcal{W},\mathcal{D}\rangle,\mathcal{N}\rangle
is a default theory associated with a normative system, then
A
is a
deontic credulous consequence
(in short, a
d
-credulous consequence) of
\mathcal{W}\cup\mathcal{W}^{\sf o}\cup\mathcal{W}^{\sf p}
if and only if
A
belongs to at least one deontic extension of
\langle\langle\mathcal{W},\mathcal{D}\rangle,\mathcal{N}\rangle
. Intuitively, the credulous agent commits herself to as many individually consistent obligations and permissions as possible.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
\langle\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F}\rangle
be a modified extension of a default theory
\langle\mathcal{W},\mathcal{D}\rangle
,
\mathcal{N}
be a normative system
\langle\langle\mathcal{W}^{\sf o},\mathcal{O}\rangle,\langle\mathcal{W}^{\sf p},\mathcal{P}\rangle\rangle
and
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
obtained from
\mathcal{N}
by extending
\mathcal{O}
or
\mathcal{P}
. For any deontic extension
\langle\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{E}}_{1},\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{E}}_{1}\rangle
of
\langle\langle\mathcal{W},\mathcal{D}\rangle,\mathcal{N}\rangle
, there exists (at least) one deontic extension
\langle\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{E}}_{2},\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{E}}_{2}\rangle
of
\langle\langle\mathcal{W},\mathcal{D}\rangle,\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\rangle
such that
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{E}}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{E}}_{2}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{E}}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{E}}_{2}
.

Proof.

We argue as in the proof of Lemma
2.1
.
∎

3.
Controlled sequent calculi for conflicting information

To design controlled sequent calculi for deontic default reasoning, we introduce the notion of
control pair
.

3.1.
Control pairs

In this subsection, we use capital Greek letters
\Gamma,\Delta,\Theta,\ldots
to denote multisets as well as
sets
of formulas (the distinction will be made explicit whenever necessary to avoid ambiguity). Boldface capital letters
{\bf S},{\bf S_{1}},\ldots...
stand for finite sets of finite sets of formulas.

A
control pair
is a pair of sets
\langle{\bf T},{\bf S}\rangle
, where
{\bf T}
collects sets of
conditions
, and
{\bf S}
collects sets of
constraints
. For their part, conditions and constraints are formulas, possibly labelled with specific superscripts
{\sf f},{\sf o},{\sf p},\ldots
to denote their role as facts, obligations, permissions – respectively. We decorate the turnstile
\vdash
with a control pair. Intuitively, this decoration expresses that the derivation of a sequent
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
must satisfy all conditions contained in each element of
{\bf T}
and respect all constraints contained in each element of
{\bf S}
. In particular, we require that
\Gamma
— possibly strengthened by additional formulas (see Definition
3.4
) — be compatible with the sets of conditions in
{\bf T}
and with the sets of constraints in
{\bf S}
, in the following sense.

Definition 3.1
.

Given a multiset
\Gamma
and a control pair
\langle{\bf T},{\bf S}\rangle
,
\Gamma
is compatible with
\langle{\bf T},{\bf S}\rangle
(in symbols
\Gamma\,||\,\langle{\bf T},{\bf S}\rangle
) if and only if for every formula
A
in
\bigcup{\bf T}
and every formula
B
in
\bigcup{\bf S}
,
\overline{\overline{\mathsf{G4^{pn}}}}
proves
\Gamma\vdash A
and refutes
\Gamma\vdash B
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
{\bf S}\subseteq{\bf S^{\prime}}
,
{\bf T}\subseteq{\bf T^{\prime}}
and the sequent
\Gamma\vdash\bigwedge\Delta
be provable in
\overline{\overline{\mathsf{G4^{pn}}}}
. The following statements about compatibility hold:

(1)

\Gamma\,||\,\langle{\bf T^{\prime}},{\bf S^{\prime}}\rangle
implies
\Gamma\,||\,\langle{\bf T},{\bf S}\rangle
;

(2)

\Delta\,||\,\langle{\bf T},\varnothing\rangle
implies
\Gamma\,||\,\langle{\bf T},\varnothing\rangle
;

(3)

\Gamma\,||\,\langle\varnothing,{\bf S}\rangle
implies
\Delta\,||\,\langle\varnothing,{\bf S}\rangle
.

Proof.

Immediate from Definition
3.1
.
∎

Let
\langle\mathcal{W},\mathcal{D}\rangle
be any default theory and
\mathcal{N}
be any normative system associated with
\langle\mathcal{W},\mathcal{D}\rangle
. We exploit control pairs to define controlled sequent calculi for
m
-credulous consequences of
\mathcal{W}
and
d
-credulous consequences of
\mathcal{W}\cup\mathcal{W}^{\sf o}\cup\mathcal{W}^{\sf p}
.

To distinguish the roles of derivable formulas, we use labelled turnstiles in sequents:

-

\mathrel{{}_{{}_{{\sf F}}}\mkern 2.0mu\rule[0.0pt]{0.4pt}{12.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\rule[0.0pt]{10.76385pt}{0.4pt}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt}\mkern 2.0mu}
for sequents tracking
m
-credulous consequences,

-

\mathrel{{}_{{}_{{\sf O}}}\mkern 2.0mu\rule[0.0pt]{0.4pt}{12.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\rule[0.0pt]{10.76385pt}{0.4pt}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt}\mkern 2.0mu}
for sequents tracking
d
-credulous consequences which correspond to obligations, and

-

\mathrel{{}_{{}_{{\sf P}}}\mkern 2.0mu\rule[0.0pt]{0.4pt}{12.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\rule[0.0pt]{10.76385pt}{0.4pt}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt}\mkern 2.0mu}
for sequents tracking
d
-credulous consequences which correspond to permissions.

When generalizing over a subset of
\{\mathrel{{}_{{}_{{\sf F}}}\mkern 2.0mu\rule[0.0pt]{0.4pt}{12.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\rule[0.0pt]{10.76385pt}{0.4pt}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt}\mkern 2.0mu}\,,\,\mathrel{{}_{{}_{{\sf O}}}\mkern 2.0mu\rule[0.0pt]{0.4pt}{12.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\rule[0.0pt]{10.76385pt}{0.4pt}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt}\mkern 2.0mu}\,,\,\mathrel{{}_{{}_{{\sf P}}}\mkern 2.0mu\rule[0.0pt]{0.4pt}{12.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\rule[0.0pt]{10.76385pt}{0.4pt}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt}\mkern 2.0mu}\}
, we use the labelled turnstile
\mathrel{{}_{{}_{{\sf\times}}}\mkern 2.0mu\rule[0.0pt]{0.4pt}{12.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\rule[0.0pt]{10.76385pt}{0.4pt}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt}\mkern 2.0mu}
.

3.2.
Control pairs for defaults

We are ready to present controlled sequent calculi for
m
-credulous consequence.

axioms and structural rules

\Gamma,A,B\mathrel{{}_{{}_{{\sf F}}}\mkern 2.0mu\rule[0.0pt]{0.4pt}{12.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 3.14027pt\hskip-2.51389pt\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 2.51389pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\rule[0.0pt]{10.76385pt}{0.4pt}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-2.51389pt\raisebox{-5.80278pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bf S}$}}\hskip-2.51389pt\hskip 2.51389pt}$}\hskip-2.51389pt\hskip-3.14027pt\raisebox{5.80278pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bf T}$}}\hskip-3.14027pt\hskip 3.14027pt}\mkern 2.0mu}\Delta

\{\Gamma\mathrel{{}_{{}_{{\sf F}}}\mkern 2.0mu\rule[0.0pt]{0.4pt}{12.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.48051pt\hskip-3.85413pt\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 3.85413pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\rule[0.0pt]{10.76385pt}{0.4pt}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-3.85413pt\raisebox{-5.80278pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bf S_{i}}$}}\hskip-3.85413pt\hskip 3.85413pt}$}\hskip-3.85413pt\hskip-4.48051pt\raisebox{6.86388pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bf T_{i}}$}}\hskip-4.48051pt\hskip 4.48051pt}\mkern 2.0mu}\Theta_{i}\}_{0\leq i\leq m}
